金融市場投資週報                 2026 年 10 月 13 日
上週重點訊息
◼ TOPIX 啟動大規模改革：日本交易所集團 10 月 7 日公布 TOPIX 調整結果，符合新制納入標
準的成分股將由 9 月底的 1,636 檔降至 986 檔，另有 35 檔股票新納入。現有成分股中，共
有 683 檔未符合流動性及浮動市值等標準，這些股票將自 2026 年 10 月底起逐步降低權
重，並於 2027 年 10 月重新評估，屆時仍未達標者將於 2028 年 7 月底降至零。此次改革有
助於改善 TOPIX 成分股的流動性及投資價值，並促使企業更加重視市場評價。

◼ 巴西總統選舉波索納洛首輪領先魯拉：巴西最高選舉法院 10 月 5 日公布首輪投票結果，參
議員弗拉維奧・波索納洛取得 47.03%的有效票，現任總統魯拉獲得 45.16%，兩人均未取得
過半票數，將於 10 月 25 日進行第二輪決選。若最終由波索納洛當選將相較有利於巴西股市
表現。
◼ IEA 支持加速釋出既有儲油：國際能源署（IEA）10 月 7 日表示，會員國支持加快釋出 3 月
已承諾提供的儲油，並將優先增加柴油供應。截至目前，會員國已釋出約 3.25 億桶儲油；若
剩餘承諾全數執行，可再向市場提供約 1 億桶供應，有助緩解運輸業與農業面臨的燃料成本
壓力。隨各國持續釋出戰略儲備石油，將有利於在短期內壓抑全球油價進一步上行。
市場展望
◼ 標普 500 獲利預估連續上修，資訊科技為主要推升來源：標普 500 企業獲利預估連續兩季上
修，其中資訊科技產業為主要改善來源，反映 AI、雲端運算及資料中心需求仍然強勁。雖然
美國資料中心仍面臨電力與基礎設施瓶頸，但建置規模持續擴大，AI 投資也逐步延伸至記憶

體、網路設備、資安及企業應用等領域。因此，預期科技類股的獲利動能仍具支撐，具 AI 供
應鏈優勢的台股與日股亦可望延續相關成長動能。
◼ 中天期債券可望兼顧息收與利率風險：美國經濟仍具韌性，加上政府與企業發債供給維持高
檔，可能使市場利率維持在較高水準。雖然投資級債信用利差位於相對低檔，但企業獲利、
現金流及償債能力仍具支撐，較高的美國公債基準利率也使其利息收益維持一定水準。短天
期債券面臨利率下降後的再投資風險，長天期債券則較易受到公債供給及利率波動影響，因
此中天期美國公債及投資級債可望在息收與利率風險之間維持較為平衡的表現。

2  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
上週重要市場資訊
⚫ 歐洲央行上周四(9/10)為年內第二次上調利率，以應對能源價格上漲帶來的通膨
壓力 ， 並警告物價壓力可能比先前預期更久 ， 推動市場加大對後續進一步收緊政
策的押注 。 歐洲央行總裁拉加德形容此次决定爲”無需多想的决定”， 並警告通膨
回落至 2%目標的時間點目前預計在 2027 年底。歐洲央行預期明年平均通膨爲
2.5%，高於 6 月的 2.3%；2027 年核心通膨預期爲 2.6%，高於此前的 2.5%。
⚫ 軍事消息人士稱，與伊朗結盟的胡塞武裝上周四(9/10)奪取葉門港口城市的控制
權 ， 並沿紅海海岸向南推進至戰略島嶼地區 。 美國總統川普表示 ， 他預計伊朗戰
爭將在美國中期選舉後結束。若胡塞武裝控制這條與 荷姆茲海峽同樣重要的水
道 ， 其支持者伊朗將在與美國的戰爭中獲得關鍵戰略優勢 ， 並進一步推高油價 。
⚫ 美國勞工部勞工統計局上週四(9/10)公布 8 月生産者物價指數 PPI 年增 5.4%，

高於市場預期的 5.3％。週五(9/11)公布 8 月消費者物價指數(CPI)年增 3.4%與前
月相同，核心 CPI 年增率 2.4%符合市場預期，月增率達 0.3%高於市場預期。
FED WATCH顯示數據公布後推升市場對於聯準會本周將升息的預期。
⚫ 日本央行數據顯示，8 月國內企業物價指數較去年同期上漲 7.6%。增幅高於市
場預估中值 7.4%。7 月漲幅經修正後爲 7.7%；8 月進口物價指數同比上漲 24.8%，
7 月修正後爲上漲 29.3%， 表明當月日圓貶值以及中東局勢影響推高進口成本和
整體通膨，同時鞏固市場對日本央行在 9 月會議進一步升息預期。

### Page 3

3  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
美元兌新台幣匯率走勢
Sep/14/2026
＜上週走勢＞
上週台幣匯價高點為 31.455，低點在 31.698，以 31.638 作收，較前一週收盤價
貶值 0.8 分。
上週台幣先升後貶，週初，受到市場對於日本央行 9 月升息的預期升溫，日圓
走升帶動美元指數跌破 99 整數價位低檔震盪 ， 加以台股高檔震盪 ， 外資延續匯入的
走勢，帶動台幣升破31.500 至 31.455 週內高點；臨近週末，美國公布8 月生產者物
價指數年增率由上個月的 4.8%上升至 5.4%，數據公布後，FEDWATCH顯示聯準會
9 月升息機率攀升至 7 成，美元指數回到 99 上方，台股高點回落，外資偏向匯出，
台幣由升轉貶至 31.698 週內低點，終場在央行調節之下收在 31.638。
＜未來分析＞
預估本週台幣區間震盪。國際市場方面，上週五公布美國 8 月核心消費者物價
指數月增率 0.3%高於市場預期的 0.2%，數據公布後，提高本週聯準會升息的機率

至接近 9 成，此外中東局勢仍有升溫風險，油價持續高檔震盪，美元指數在 99 整數
關口盤整；就台灣市場來看，台灣 8 月出口衝上 824 億美元，創下歷年單月新高紀
錄，月增 9.4%，年增 41%，展現極為強勁的成長動能。顯示受惠於 AI 需求全面爆
發，帶動外銷出口暢旺，基本面強勁持續支撐台幣的表現；盤勢上，匯價逢低吸引
進口商及政府基金買盤進場，逢高則面臨出口商賣美金，且本週台美均有利率決策
會議，預估匯率波動將加劇。綜合以上，預估本週台幣走勢區間震盪。
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌台幣：31.400-31.900

### Page 4

4  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌人民幣匯率走勢  
Sep/14/2026 
＜上週走勢＞ 
    上週人民幣震盪走升。週初，雖然美伊戰火未平，帶動油價及美債利率高漲，
但市場對於日本央行 9 月升息預期升溫，日圓偏升帶動美元指數跌破 99 價位後低
檔震盪，人民幣延續偏升走勢，走升至6.7022 週內高點；臨近週末，美國公布8 月
生產者物價指數 5.4%優於市場預期 5.3%， 加以8 月核心消費者物價指數月增率 0.3%
優於市場預期的 0.2%，數據公布後，FEDWATCH顯示本週聯準會 9 月升息預期大
幅攀升至近 9 成，美元指數回到99 上方，人民幣回吐部分週初以來的漲幅，終場境
外人民幣收在 6.7071，週線升值 8 點，境內人民幣則收在6.7079，週線升值 29 點。  
＜未來分析＞ 
     預估本週人民幣區間偏升。國際市場方面，美國通膨數據表現優於市場預期，
加以油價攀升至每桶 100 美元上方 ， 美債殖利率位居高檔 ， 美元指數也在99 上方盤
整，關注本週聯準會最新利率決議，預計在會議結果出爐以前，聯準會升息預期將
支撐美元指數，限制人民幣升值的空間。中國國內方面，人行中間價格緩步偏升，
上週升破 6.7800 價位，持續釋放人民幣偏強信號，支撐人民幣延續升值的方向。技
術面方面 ，USD/CNH 匯價居於各天期均線下方 ，且均線向下發散 ，加以匯價由低點
向上反彈測試 6.7150 關鍵價位後面臨壓力，顯示空方動能較盛，預計人民幣將延續
升值的態勢。綜合以上，預估本週人民幣走勢區間偏升。

＜預估本週＞ 美元兌人民幣：6.6800-6.7300

### Page 5

5  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌日圓匯率走勢  
         Sep/14/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
    上週 USD/JPY 震盪下跌 。週初 ，因市場預期日本央行將加快升息步伐及資金將
回流日本，使 USD/JPY 跌破至 155 整數關卡之下；週間，USD/JPY 延續跌勢最低
來到 152.87 後反彈，後續大致在 153 至 154 間波動；臨近週末，美國公布 8 月 PPI
及 CPI 數據顯示通膨仍處高位 ，USD/JPY 一度反彈至154.6 後回落 ， 終場收在153.54，
週線大跌 270 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 USD/JPY 區間震盪，主要原因如下：一、上週公布日本第二季 GDP
較上季增長 0.4%，季增年率也由 1.1%上升至 1.4%，主因為企業資本支出改善，而
7 月名目薪資年增 4.7%為 30 年來最大漲幅，而實質薪資年增 2.4%也為 5 年多來最
大漲幅，顯示日本經濟復甦力道較原先預期強勁，進一步強化市場對於日央升息的
預期，有利日圓表現。二、美國 8 月 CPI 及 PPI 數據顯示美國通膨仍處高位，強化
市場對聯準會升息預期，且中東局勢升溫，帶動油價突破 100 美元，預期將支撐美
元，限縮 USD/JPY 下行空間。三、就技術面而言，日線圖中，USD/JPY 匯價多次向
下測試 153 整數關卡未能有效突破，顯示下方有買盤支撐，而 155 關卡附近則為前
波壓力位，因此短線先以區間看待。綜合以上，預估USD/JPY 本週呈現區間震盪的
可能性較高。 
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌日幣：152.00-157.00 
前週交易區間 
152.87 156.27

6  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌歐元匯率走勢  
         Sep/14/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
    上週 EUR/USD 小幅下跌。 週初 ，EUR/USD 大致在 1.1600 至 1.1635 窄幅波動；
週間，市場等待歐洲央行利率決議及 美國通膨數據，EUR/USD 持續在 1.1615 至
1.1653 波動；週四晚間公布歐洲央行利率決議如預期升息 1 碼，而美國 8 月 PPI 高
於預期使市場對聯準會本月升息預期上升，EUR/USD 下行跌破 1.16 整數關口；臨
近週末，美國公布 8 月 CPI 顯示通膨仍具韌性，帶動 EUR/USD 走低來至 1.1568，
終場收在 1.1598，週線下跌 15 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 EUR/USD 區間震盪，主要原因如下：一、上週歐洲央行公布利率決
議如預期升息一碼，總裁拉加德表示即使面對能源價格衝擊，歐洲經濟第二季依然
展現韌性，預期第三季仍會延續，但未來通膨仍面臨上行風險，偏鷹派言論使市場
對於歐洲央行未來持續升息的預期上升，將帶給歐元支撐。二、美國 8 月 PPI 月增
0.4%符合市場預期 ， 年增5.4%高於預期 ，而 8 月 CPI 月增 0.4%年增 3.4%符合預期 ，
顯示通膨仍維持高位，使市場對聯準會升息預期增加，將支撐美元，不利歐元匯價
上行。三、技術面而言，在日線圖中，EUR/USD 匯價雖一度突破 1.1630 年線但未
能站穩，顯示上方仍有賣壓，而下方1.1550 附近則有支撐，在有效突破前以區間震
盪看待。綜合以上，預估 EUR/USD 本週呈現區間震盪的可能性較高。 
＜預估本週＞ 歐元兌美元：1.1530-1.1680 
前週交易區間 
1.1568 1.1653

7  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
                         新台幣利率走勢分析

115.09.07-115.09.11 
銀行隔夜拆款利率 0.805% ~ 0.830%  
30 天期短票利率   1.66% ~ 1.69% 
公債－10 年期-A15106 1.92 % ~ 1.921% 
－ 5 年期- A15108 1.822% ~ 1.823%

一、 台幣貨幣市場部分 ，本週央行連日減發定存單 ， 使市場資金呈現平穩態勢 ， 且
資金需求不若先前，銀行間交投較為清淡，令短率持穩於近期區間。 
週 五 隔 夜 拆 款 利 率 區 間 在 0.805%~0.830% ， 30 天 期 短 票 主 要 成 交 在
1.67%~1.69%，利率較上週上升 1 個基本點。

1.92%。 
美伊衝突升溫，帶動國際油價持續走揚，加劇市場對通膨之疑慮，加上美國8
月非農就業人數大增、8 月 PPI 年增率高於市場預期，市場預估聯準會下週升
息機率升至七成，帶動美債利率大幅反彈；台灣央行將於下週召開理監事會
議 ， 在台灣經濟成長與通膨維持高位下 ， 台灣央行面臨緊縮壓力 ， 預期台債殖
利率面臨走升壓力 ， 預估5 年券區間為 1.83%~1.90%，10 年券區間為 1.93%~2%。

HTC Corp. (2498.TW): XR headset and AI glasses to support growth 
ahead; Near-term cost pressures remain; Sell
10 October 2026 | 12:24AM HKT
In September, HTC accelerated its global wearable expansion with the commercial 
launch of the new round-frame edition of its VIVE Eagle AI smart glasses alongside 
substantial software enhancements across North America, Europe, and Australia 
(news). Features include hands-free image capture, first-person POV live streaming, 
real-time AI translation, AI Notes, dashcam utility, and assistive vision for the visually 
impaired. Meanwhile, the company continues to anchor its VR headset strategy 
around commercial enterprise solutions, underscored by its flagship deployment 
with Axon which shifts hazardous tactical training into virtual environments to 
reduce operational costs, complemented by use cases in complex surgical 
simulation, interior space visualization, and virtual meeting rooms. 
While we maintain our constructive view on HTC’s long-term growth trajectory 
backed by sustained product innovation, we are Sell rated given near-term earnings 
headwinds - specifically core monetization drivers across XR/ VR/ AI glasses and 
software will require extended lead time to ramp adoption, compounded by 
consumer electronics demand softness and margin compression from elevated 
memory component costs. We would turn more positive if we see 
stronger-than-expected XR headset/ AI glasses growth driven by market demand, 
application development or customer expansion.  
Earnings revisions: We factor in 3Q26 revenues and revise down 2026-28E net 
income. We revise down 2026-28E revenues by 4%/ 0%/ 27% due to demand 
pressures of consumer electronics (impacted by soaring memory cost) and soft 
demand of XR products in the near-term. GM estimates are largely unchanged in 
2026-2027E, and we revise down our OPM estimates by 2.1/ 0.3ppts on a smaller 
business scale. GM are revised up by 5.7ppts in 2028E to reflect mix upgrade to VR 
hardware and software with high margin.

### Page 2

Valuation: With earnings revisions and an unchanged valuation methodology, our 12M 
target price is revised down to NT$32.8 (vs. previously at NT$34.4). We continue to 
derive our target price via 85% based on a fundamental valuation and 15% from a 
theoretical M&A valuation. (1) Our fundamental valuation is based on 2028E P/E of 
19.3x (unchanged), discounted back to 2026E at an 11.5% COE. (2) Our M&A valuation is 
based on 23.6x 2028E P/E (unchanged) then discounted back to 2026E at an 11.5% 
COE. 
Our target 2028E discounted P/E multiple of 19.3x is in line with XR peers’ historical avg. 
P/E at 19.3x in 2022 (method unchanged, Exhibit 5). HTC’s 2029 NI growth will be 15% 
YoY per our estimate, which is lower than XR peers’ (Meta/ Goertek/ Microsoft/ Apple/ 
Sony) 2023-24 avg. NI growth at 23% YoY. Our M&A target multiple is 23.6x 2028E P/E, 
which is derived from the peers’ historical avg. +1std P/E in 2022 (methodology 
unchanged). We use avg. +1std P/E of peers to reflect the M&A premium.  
Our 12M target price of NT$32.8 implies 4.1x 2026E P/S vs. 126% revenue YoY growth 
on average in 2026E-27E. The implied 2026E P/S of 4.1x is lower than the company’s 
current price implied 2026E P/S at 4.9x, showing that valuation is stretched. Maintain 
Sell.

### Page 3

Price Target Risks and Methodology - HTC Corp. 
Valuation methodology: We are Sell rated on HTC with a 12M target price of NT$32.8, 
which is derived 85% from a fundamental valuation and 15% from a theoretical M&A 
valuation. (1) Our fundamental valuation is based on 19.3x 2028E P/E, discounted back 
to 2026E at an 11.5% COE. The 19.3x target P/E multiple is derived from peers’ avg. P/E 
in 2022; (2) Our M&A valuation is based on 23.6x 2028E P/E and discounted back to 
2026E at an 11.5% COE. 
Key risks: 1) Better-than-expected VR demand; 2) lower-than-expected competition in 
VR headsets; and 3) lower-than-expected price pressure from peers.

on an Investment List is determined by a stock’s total return potential relative to its coverage universe. Any stock not assigned as a Buy or a Sell on an
Investment List with an active rating (i.e., a  stock that is not Rating Suspended, Not Rated, Early-Stage Biotech, Coverage Suspended or Not Covered), is

deemed Neutral. Each region manages Regional Conviction Lists, which are selected from Buy rated stocks on the respective region’s Investment Lists
and represent investment recommendations focused on the size of the total return potential and/or the likelihood of the realization of the return across
their respective areas of coverage.  The addition or removal of stocks from such Conviction Lists are managed by the Investment Review Committee or
other designated committee in each respective region and do not represent a change in the analysts’ investment rating for such stocks.

Total return potential represents the upside or downside differential between the current share price and the price target, including all paid or
anticipated dividends, expected during the time horizon associated with the price target. Price targets are required for all covered stocks. The total
return potential, price target and associated time horizon are stated in each report adding or reiterating an Investment List membership.

Update
Delta Electronics Inc.  | Asia Pacific
Our Thoughts on USITC
Investigation No. 337
The near-term impact should be manageable, but we'll closely
monitor developments on this matter.
What's new:
• The US International Trade Commission (USITC) formally launched a patent
infringement investigation on October 9, 2026, following a complaint by
Vicor Corporation, a US-based power electronics company, against 20
entities, including Delta and others across the power supply, server assembly

and semiconductor space.
• Vicor alleges that certain imported vertical power delivery systems (used
mostly in HPC chips) have infringed US Patent 10,903,734.
• Vicor requested two types of remedy: 1) a Limited Exclusion Order (LEO);
and 2) Cease and Desist Orders (CDOs), which could restrict US imports and
sales of infringing products if granted.
Our view:
• We note that this is not the first time Delta and Vicor have had legal
disputes over patent infringement.

• We will closely monitor any developments in this matter, though we think
the impact on Delta's operations should be manageable (the products in
dispute account for high single digit % of its total revenue for 2026,  based
on our estimates).
• We remain OW.

2023-07-12 Vicor v. Delta et al. | E.D. Tex., 2:23-cv-00323 Patent infringement: US 6,930,893; 9,166,481; 9,516,761; 10,199,950. Settled; dismissed with prejudice (May 13, 2025).
2023-07-12 Vicor v. Delta, Cyntec, Foxconn, Quanta et al. | ITC 337-TA-1370 ITC import complaint: US 9,166,481; 9,516,761; 10,199,950. Closed (Feb 13, 2025); partial infringement finding; LEO/CDOs issued.

2023-10-23 Delta v. Vicor | W.D. Tex., 6:23-cv-00726 Patent infringement: US 9,819,263 (power converters). Settled; dismissed with prejudice (May 7, 2025).
2023-11-01 Delta v. Vicor | D. Del., 1:23-cv-01246 Patent infringement: US 8,711,580 and 10,877,534. Settled; dismissed with prejudice (May 8, 2025).
2024-01-02 Delta v. Vicor | China patent case (number unverified) Patent infringement: CN 106571354 B (power-converter manufacturing). Final case disposition not confirmed.

2024-01-26 Delta (applicant) / Vicor | D. Del., 1:24-cv-00107 Section 1782 evidence request for China patent litigation. Jan 10, 2025: main discovery request denied; limited relief granted.
2026-01-09 Vicor v. Delta | E.D. Tex., 2:26-cv-00017 Patent infringement: US 12,395,087 (bus-converter design). Stayed (Feb 24, 2026) pending ITC 337-TA-1484.
2026-01-12 Vicor v. Delta et al. | ITC 337-TA-1484 ITC import complaint: US 12,395,087 (power modules and servers). Ongoing; instituted Feb 11, 2026 (published Feb 17); target completion Sep 20, 2027.

2026-07-23 Delta v. Vicor | W.D. Tex., 1:26-cv-02057 Alleged trade-secret misuse and interference with business relations. Ongoing; no merits ruling reported.
2026-07-24 Delta Shanghai v. Vicor | D. Del., 1:26-cv-00913 Patent infringement: US 10,845,853 (processor-board power delivery). Ongoing; no final infringement ruling reported.
2026-07-31 Delta v. Vicor | W.D. Tex., 7:26-cv-00288 Patent infringement: US 11,923,772 (DC/DC converters). Pending; Vicor moved to dismiss or transfer (Sep 28, 2026).

2026-09-08 Vicor v. Delta, Infineon et al. | E.D. Tex., 2:26-cv-00796 Patent infringement: US 10,903,734 (vertical power delivery). Ongoing; early stage, no infringement finding.
2026-09-08 Delta (applicant) / Vicor | D. Del., 1:26-cv-01135 Section 1782 evidence request for overseas proceedings. Pending; no final order confirmed.
2026-09-09 Vicor v. Delta et al. | ITC 337-TA-1526 ITC import complaint: US 10,903,734 (vertical power delivery). Instituted (Oct 9, 2026); no final ruling.

Morgan Stanley Research 3
Valuation Methodology and Risks
Delta Electronics Inc. (2308.TW)
Base case: P/E multiple of 30x based on average 2027-28 EPS.                          We believe our target multiple
is justified by the 63% earnings CAGR we estimate for 2025-28, implying a PEG of 0.5, versus
the stock's ~23x P/E and 23% earnings CAGR during 2022-25, which implied a PEG of 1.0.
Risks to Upside
n Faster-than-expected global economic growth

n Falling raw material prices
n Margin improvement
Risks to Downside
n Global economic slowdown
n Ongoing component supply tightness
n Rising raw material prices
n Margin contraction as a result of higher operating expenses or faster ASP erosion

STOCK RATINGS
Morgan Stanley uses a relative rating system using terms such as Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated or Underweight (see definitions below). Morgan Stanley does not assign ratings of Buy,
Hold or Sell to the stocks we cover. Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight are not the equivalent of buy, hold and sell.  Investors should carefully read the definitions of all
ratings used in Morgan Stanley Research. In addition, since Morgan Stanley Research contains more complete information concerning the analyst's views, investors should carefully read Morgan

Stanley Research, in its entirety, and not infer the contents from the rating alone.  In any case, ratings (or research) should not be used or relied upon as investment advice.  An investor's decision
to buy or sell a stock should depend on individual circumstances (such as the investor's existing holdings) and other considerations.

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.
Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia -
relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.

### Page 1

ASE Technology Holding

3Q26 sales outperformance affirms solid 
transformation progress

Reiterate Rating: BUY | PO: 900.00 TWD | Price: 762.00 TWD

3Q26 sales beat on LEAP ramp and mature ASP strength 
ASE reported 3Q26 sales +28% QoQ better than its guide, driven by stronger IC ATM 
(+20% QoQ vs. BofAe +13% QoQ) and mild upside in EMS (+42% QoQ vs. BofAe +40% 
QoQ). We believe IC ATM strength is led by modest mainstream packaging and testing 
price hike on industry supply tightness, supplemented by LEAP business ramp. EMS also 
benefited from Apple product seasonal builds and higher component cost pass through. 
With improving packaging and testing utilization and ASP and rising LEAP mix, we 
expect its IC ATM GMs to improve 300bps QoQ to 30% (vs. high-20% guide) though 
consolidated GMs (+180bps QoQ to 23%) could be partially offset by EMS outgrowth. 
AI pipeline diversification anchors capex plan in ’26-28 
We model ASE’s 4Q26 IC ATM sales +7-9% QoQ as its LEAP opportunity diversifying 
from TSMC outsourcing to direct project wins including AMD’s Venice CPU, putting sales 
on track to +42% YoY in ’26 (vs. its +35% guide). Its IC ATM GMs could further expand 
to 31% as LEAP reaching 30% of its sales (vs. 18%/22% in 1H/3Q26). While we expect 
ASE to spend US$13bn+ annual capex in ’27-28 (vs. US$10.5bn in ’26), the accelerating 
IC ATM sales (BofAe +40% CAGR from ’26-28) on advanced expansion and mature price 
uplift, along with the business mix upgrade to cloud AI/HPC projects carrying 35-45% 
GMs should offset hiking depreciation (BofAe +39% CAGR from ’26-28), lifting its IC 
ATM GM from 29% in ’26 to low 30% in ’27 and mid 30% in ’28 with operating leverage. 
Stronger earnings quality unlocks re-rating potential 
We lift our ’26/27/28E EPS to NT$20/NT$34/NT$50 on strong LEAP outlook. Keep Buy 
rating and raise POs from NT$800 (US$51) to NT$900 (US$58) still on 20x 2H27-1H28 
P/E (vs. its 5-26x range) backed by its structural business transformation with better 
sales and GMs growth profile as the industry supply further consolidates amid higher 
tech entry barrier.

Company Sector 
Semiconductors

Company Description 
Established in 1984 and headquartered in Taiwan, ASE 
Technology Holding is the no.1 outsourced assembly and 
testing (OSAT) firm globally, in terms of market share by 
revenue. ASE acquired Siliconware Precision (SPIL) in 2018, 
and had acquired USI in 2010, which became its EMS 
business segment providing synergies in system-level 
packaging. 2 shares = 1 ADR.

Investment Rationale 
We rate ASE Buy, factoring in the company's opportunity in 
advanced packaging and testing evidenced in TSMC's 
outsourcing and its own heavy capex investment. The 
strategy shift should allow the company to capture fast 
growing addressable market in high performance 
computing applications and improves its margins. ASE 
could maintain its leading position in the Outsourced 
Semiconductor Assembly and Test (OSAT) universe, owing 
to its well-established footprints and widespread service 
offerings.

### Page 5

Price objective basis & risk 
ASE Technology Holding (XSRIF / ASX) 
We value ASE Technology Holding at NT$900 per share (US$58 for ADR), based on 20x 
2H27-1H28E P/E, above its historical range of 5-26x, in view of its solidified industry 
position in AI era. We use P/E ratio to value ASE as the company has been able to 
maintain healthy profitability through the cycles in the past 20 years due to 
consolidating industry landscape supporting a more benign supply/demand and keeping 
pricing and margins less cyclical.

Downside risks to our PO are 1) share loss and/or ASP erosion owing to Chinese players' 
cannibalization and/or ASE's worsening execution, 2) weakening end demand due to 
uncontrollable matters, such as macro halt and/or geopolitical tensions, 3) regulatory 
issues that could hinder ASE from generating synergies and benefit its competitors.

9 月经济数据点评：生产偏强、需求渐进修复

[Table_Author] 
 魏争 分析师  沈夏宜 分析师

Email:weizheng@lczq.com Email:shenxiayi@lczq.com  
证书:S1320524100001 证书:S1320523020004

### Page 2

宏观经济点评  
请务必阅读正文之后的信息披露和法律声明 2 
6.2%升至 9.8%，我们理解部分原因是地缘局势提高能源安全和保供要
求，国内油气增储上产持续推进，为采矿业生产提供了支撑 。二是电热燃
水业增速回升。电力、热力、燃气及水生产和供应业增加值增长 5.6%，
较 8 月提高 0.7 个百分点。从实物产量看，核电、风电和太阳能发电分别
增长 21.5%、10.6%和 19.5%。 
服务业生产明显改善，生产性服务业延续支撑，生活性服务有修复。9
月，服务业生产指数同比增长 4.8%，较 8 月提高 0.7 个百分点；一是数
字信息和生产配套型服务业继续保持较快增长。 信息传输软件和信息技术
服务业、租赁和商务服务业、交通运输仓储和邮政业生产指数 分别增长
9.8%、9.5%和 5.4%。二是服务业改善广度有提升。住宿和餐饮业生产指
数增长 5.3%，服务业商务活动指数由 49.3%升至 50.2%，重新进入扩张
区间；电信广播电视及卫星传输、货币金融和保险等行业商务活动指数均
位于 55%以上1。 
投资低位企稳。9 月，固定资产投资累计同比下降 7.2%，降幅与 8 月持
平。按累计金额反推，9 月当月投资同比约下降 7.2%，较 8 月的下降
11.0%明显收窄；剔除房地产后，当月投资同比约下降 3.4%，降幅较 8
月收窄约 4.4 个百分点。季调环比增长 0.07%，是 3 月以来首次转正。 
制造业投资小幅修复，增长仍集中在少数优势领域 。9 月，制造业累计投
资下降 2.1%，降幅较 1—8 月收窄 0.2 个百分点。结构上看，一是正增行
业仍主要集中在装备和新兴制造领域。国家统计局公布的 13 个制造业行
业中，只有 5 个保持正增长，其中铁路船舶航空航天、计算机通信电子设
备制造业投资分别增长 15.8%和 9.6%，纺织、电气机械和通用设备制造
业分别增长 4.2%、1.2%和 1.1%。高技术产业投资增长 4.9%，其中信息
服务、航空航天器及设备、电子及通信设备制造业投资分别增长 20.2%、
12.5%和 7.6%。二是上游原材料和下游消费制造业投资仍较弱。农副食
品加工、食品制造、化工、医药、有色金属冶炼、金属制品、专用设备和
汽车制造业投资分别下降 2.5%、10.3%、7.5%、8.1%、5.1%、10.1%、
8.9%和 6.3%。 
地产继续承压，价格端修复难抵销售走弱。9 月，地产投资累计同比下降
20.6%，降幅较 8 月扩大 0.7 个百分点；按累计金额反推，9 月当月投资
下降约 26.3%，降幅较 8 月扩大约 0.8 个百分点。结构上看，一是地产销
售再次走弱，约束投资预期。 商品房销售面积和销售额累计分别下降
13.2%和 13.9%，降幅较 1—8 月扩大 1.1 和 0.9 个百分点；按累计金额反
推，9 月当月销售面积和销售额分别下降约 20.7%和 20.1%，较 8 月的下
降 14.8%和 12.1%明显走弱。东部地区销售额降幅由 12.6%扩大至
13.4%。30 大中城市商品房成交面积同比下降 10.9%，较 8 月扩大约 7.5
个百分点，其中一线城市增速由 18.8%降至 2.4%，二线城市降幅由
15.8%扩大至 22.7%，三线城市降幅基本持平。二是供给和资金端仍然偏
弱。新开工面积累计下降 24.9%，降幅较 8 月扩大 0.1 个百分点，竣工面
积降幅由 23.7%扩大至 25.1%。房企到位资金下降 21.6%，降幅扩大 0.6

### Page 3

宏观经济点评  
请务必阅读正文之后的信息披露和法律声明 3 
个百分点，其中，自筹资金降幅由 20.0%扩大至 21.4%；国内贷款和个人
按揭贷款降幅则分别小幅收窄至 33.1%和 22.1%。三是价格端有积极变
化。9 月，70 城新房和二手房价格同比分别下降 3.0%和 4.8%，降幅均较
8 月收窄 0.3 个百分点；环比看，新房降幅由 0.2%收窄至 0.1%，二手房
下降 0.3%，与上月持平。一线城市新房和二手房价格分别上涨 0.1%和
0.2%，二线城市新房价格由下降转为持平、二手房降幅收窄。 
基建投资继续放缓，项目端景气 有改善迹象。9 月，基建投资累计同比下
降 4.2%，降幅较 8 月扩大 0.2 个百分点。一方面，部分传统基建领域仍
然偏弱。交通运输仓储和邮政业投资增长 1.9%，较前期回落 0.2 个百分
点；水利环境和公共设施管理业投资下降 12.8%，降幅扩大 1.8 个百分
点，其中水利管理和公共设施管理业均下降 12%以上。电热燃水投资下降
6.8%，降幅较前期小幅收窄。另一方面，项目储备和部分结构性领域出现
积极信号，但向施工和实物工作量的传导仍不充分。9 月，建筑业商务活
动指数由 46.9%升至 50.3%，从业人员和业务活动预期指数也有所提高。
信息传输、航空运输和水上运输投资分别增长 30.8%、13.8%和 13.5%，
交通、能源、数据等“六张网”相关领域仍有支撑。 建筑业新订单指数由
42.4%升至 45.7%，尚未回到扩张区间，指向前端项目需求有改善，但向
施工和实物工作量的传导仍不充分。 
消费是内需端改善最明显的分项，商品消费仍受汽车拖累 。9 月，社零同
比增长 1.1%，较 8 月提高 0.7 个百分点，环比增长 0.35%，剔除汽车后
社零同比增长 3.9%，较 8 月加快 1.4 个百分点。结构上看，商品和餐饮
消费同步恢复。9 月，商品零售额增长 1.0%，较 8 月提高 0.7 个百分点；
餐饮收入增长 2.1%，提高 1.0 个百分点。从更广义的消费口径看，服务
消费仍是消费的重要支撑。三季度，社会消费商品和服务零售总额增长
2.6%，其中服务零售额增长 5.0%，明显高于商品零售额的 1.0%；网上
服务零售额增长 5.3%。通讯信息、旅游咨询租赁和文体休闲服务零售额
保持较快增长。商品消费方面，必选消费和通讯器材维持韧性，汽车是主
要拖累。服装、化妆品、日用品、家电和中西药品零售额分别增长
1.2%、5.4%、4.1%、1.9%和 5.0%，通讯器材类增长 31.1%。汽车零售
额下降 21.2%，降幅较 8 月扩大 2.7 个百分点；家具、建筑装潢材料仍分
别下降 3.0%和 9.2%，降幅较 8 月有所收窄。 
展望后续，四季度经济有望延续生产较强、需求渐进修复的格局，未来增
量支撑主要来自两条线索。总体看，三季度实际增长边际企稳，优势制造
业、外需和服务消费构成主要支撑。往后看，增量支撑线索有两方面。一
方面，以“六张网”和 8000 亿元新型政策性金融工具为抓手，加快资金
投放和项目开工。9 月以来三家政策性银行投放的新型政策性金融工具资
金已经超过 1000 亿元2，建筑业 PMI 和新订单指数同步回升，指向政策
正在向项目端传导。不过，从补充资本金、落实配套融资到形成施工和实
物工作量仍存在时滞，基建对传统建筑链的拉动或更多在四季度后半段逐
步显现。另一方面，服务消费仍是扩大内需更具确定性的方向。9 月多部
门出台金融支持服务业扩能提质政策，叠加扩大消费“十五五”规划对养

### Page 4

宏观经济点评  
请务必阅读正文之后的信息披露和法律声明 4 
老、托育、文旅、健康等领域的部署，服务消费有望继续快于商品消费。

风险提示：外需边际走弱、地缘政治超预期恶化、AI 产业链降温超预期、
政策资金投放节奏不及预期。

宏观经济点评  
请务必阅读正文之后的信息披露和法律声明 7 
1. GDP：实际增长边际回升 
实际 GDP 边际回升， 名义增长继续快于实际增长。三季度经济增长呈现三个特征，
一是实际增长较二季度有改善。 三季度实际GDP 同比增长 4.5%， 高于二季度的4.3%，
但仍略低于上半年平均水平；前三季度 GDP 累计增长 4.6%，较上半年回落 0.1 个百分
点。分产业看，实际 GDP 回升主要来自第二产业改善。第二产业增加值增速由二季度
的 3.0%回升至 3.4%， 第三产业由5.1%小幅升至 5.2%， 第一产业则由3.7%回落至 3.6%。
二是名义 GDP 继续快于实际 GDP，第二产业价格修复仍是重要支撑。 根据现价计算，
三季度名义 GDP 同比增长约 6.0%，较二季度提高 0.1 个百分点，高于实际 GDP 增速
1.5 个百分点。按名义与实际增速差近似估算，三季度 GDP 平减指数约为 1.5%，较二
季度的 1.6%略有回落， 指向整体价格修复仍在延续， 但没有进一步加快。 结构上， 第二
产业价格为主要支撑。二季度，第一产业平减指数由-3.3%回升至-1.7%，第三产业则由
1.3%回落至 0.7%， 第二产业平减指数约3.0%升至 3.6%， 其中工业由3.8%上升至 4.4%，
其表现与国际能源价格上涨以及 AI 产业链、 有色和电子设备价格较强基本一致。三是内
需端对经济增长的支撑边际回落，其中投资为最大拖累，外需端支撑边际增强。 支出法
看， 前三季度最终消费支出对经济增长的贡献率为45.7%， 较上半年回落0.3 个百分点；
资本形成总额贡献率为 28.3%，较上半年回落 7.8 个百分点；货物和服务净出口贡献率
为 26.1%，较上半年提升 8.2 个百分点。

2. 生产：工业继续回升 
工业生产继续回升，采矿业和电热燃水业改善显著。9 月，规上工业增加值同比增
长 5.5%，较 8 月提高 0.3 个百分点，环比增长 0.6%。总量上看，出口对工业生产的拉
动延续。9 月，出口交货值同比增长 17.0%，较 8 月提高 5.9 个百分点。结构上看，工
业生产回升有两方面原因。一方面，制造业增加值维持韧性，高增长仍集中在装备制造
和高技术制造领域。9 月，制造业增加值增长 6.0%，较 8 月小幅回落 0.1 个百分点，但
仍高于整体工业增速。其中，高技术制造业增长 15.3%；金属制品、通用设备、专用设
备、 铁路船舶航空航天、 电气机械和计算机通信电子设备制造业分别增长 8.2%、8.5%、
12.1%、11.7%、8.1%和 16.4%。下游消费制造业总体保持正增。农副食品加工、食品
制造、 酒饮料和精制茶、 纺织、 医药和汽车制造业分别增长4.1%、7.3%、5.5%、3.2%、
4.9%和 6.7%。另一方面，拖累项增加值边际修复。一是采矿业由负转正，对工业生产
的拖累明显减轻。9 月，采矿业增加值由下降 1.4%转为增长 1.3%，其中煤炭开采和洗
选业降幅由 5.6%收窄至 3.3%，石油和天然气开采业增速由 6.2%升至 9.8%，我们理解
部分原因是地缘局势提高能源安全和保供要求，国内油气 增储上产持续推进，为采矿业
生产提供了支撑。二是电热燃水业增速回升。电力、热力、燃气及水生产和供应业增加
值增长 5.6%，较 8 月提高 0.7 个百分点。 从实物产量看， 核电、 风电和太阳能发电分别
增长 21.5%、10.6%和 19.5%。 
服务业生产明显改善，生产性服务业延续支撑，生活性服务有修复。 9 月，服务业
生产指数同比增长 4.8%，较8 月提高 0.7 个百分点； 一是数字信息和生产配套型服务业
继续保持较快增长。信息传输软件和信息技术服务业、租赁和商务服务业、交通运输仓
储和邮政业生产指数分别增长 9.8%、9.5%和 5.4%。二是服务业改善广度有提升。 住宿
和餐饮业生产指数增长 5.3%，服务业商务活动指数由 49.3%升至 50.2%，重新进入扩
张区间； 电信广播电视及卫星传输、 货币金融和保险等行业商务活动指数均位于55%以
上。 
图5 采矿业规上增加值显著回升（%）

图6 规上工业增加值环比高于季节性（%）

3. 投资：延续回落 
投资低位企稳。9 月， 固定资产投资累计同比下降7.2%， 降幅与 8 月持平。按累计
金额反推，9 月当月投资同比约下降 7.2%，较 8 月的下降 11.0%明显收窄； 剔除房地产
后， 当月投资同比约下降3.4%， 降幅较8 月收窄约 4.4 个百分点。 季调环比增长0.07%，
是 3 月以来首次转正。 
图8 固定资产投资低位企稳（%）

### Page 10

宏观经济点评  
请务必阅读正文之后的信息披露和法律声明 10 
制造业投资小幅修复， 增长仍集中在少数优势领域。9 月， 制造业累计投资下降2.1%，
降幅较 1—8 月收窄 0.2 个百分点。 结构上看， 一是正增行业仍主要集中在装备和新兴制
造领域。国家统计局公布的 13 个制造业行业中，只有 5 个保持正增长，其中铁路船舶
航空航天、 计算机通信电子设备制造业投资分别增长15.8%和 9.6%， 纺织、 电气机械和
通用设备制造业分别增长 4.2%、1.2%和 1.1%。高技术产业投资增长 4.9%，其中信息
服务、 航空航天器及设备、 电子及通信设备制造业投资分别增长20.2%、12.5%和 7.6%。
二是上游原材料和下游消费制造业投资仍较弱。 农副食品加工、 食品制造、 化工、 医药、
有色金属冶炼、 金属制品、 专用设备和汽车制造业投资分别下降2.5%、10.3%、7.5%、
8.1%、5.1%、10.1%、8.9%和 6.3%。

4. 消费：改善相对明显，服务强于商品 
消费是内需端改善最明显的分项， 商品消费仍受汽车拖累。9 月， 社零同比增长1.1%，
较 8 月提高 0.7 个百分点，环比增长 0.35%，剔除汽车后社零同比增长 3.9%，较 8 月
加快 1.4 个百分点。 结构上看， 商品和餐饮消费同步恢复。9 月， 商品零售额增长1.0%，
较 8 月提高 0.7 个百分点； 餐饮收入增长2.1%， 提高1.0 个百分点。 从更广义的消费口
径看， 服务消费仍是消费的重要支撑。 三季度， 社会消费商品和服务零售总额增长2.6%，
其中服务零售额增长 5.0%， 明显高于商品零售额的 1.0%； 网上服务零售额增长5.3%。
通讯信息、旅游咨询租赁和文体休闲服务零售额保持较快增长。商品消费方面，必选消
费和通讯器材维持韧性，汽车是主要拖累。服装、化妆品、日用品、家电和中西药品零
售额分别增长 1.2%、5.4%、4.1%、1.9%和 5.0%，通讯器材类增长 31.1%。汽车零售
额下降 21.2%， 降幅较8 月扩大 2.7 个百分点； 家具、 建筑装潢材料仍分别下降3.0%和
9.2%，降幅较 8 月有所收窄。

5. 展望：有望延续生产较强、内需修复格局 
展望后续，四季度经济有望延续生产较强、需求渐进修复的格局，未来增量支撑主
要来自两条线索。总体看，三季度实际增长边际企稳，优势制造业、外需和服务消费构
成主要支撑。 往后看， 增量支撑线索有两方面。 一方面， 以 “六张网” 和8000 亿元新型
政策性金融工具为抓手，加快资金投放和项目开工。9 月以来三家政策性银行投放的新
型政策性金融工具资金已经超过 1000 亿元，建筑业 PMI 和新订单指数同步回升，指向
政策正在向项目端传导。不过，从补充资本金、落实配套融资到形成施工和实物工作量
仍存在时滞，基建对传统建筑链的拉动或更多在四季度后半段逐步显现。另一方面，服
务消费仍是扩大内需更具确定性的方向。9 月多部门出台金融支持服务业扩能提质政策，
叠加扩大消费“十五五”规划对养老、托育、文旅、健康等领域的部署，服务消费有望
继续快于商品消费。

及预期。

投资评级说明 
投资建议的评级标准  评级 说明 
评级标准为报告发布日后的 6 个月内公司股价 （或行业
指数） 相对同期基准指数的相对市场表现。 其中A 股市
场以沪深 300 指数为基准； 新三板市场以三板成指 （针
对协议转让标的） 或三板做市指数 （针对做市转让标的）
为基准；香港市场以恒生指数为基准。 
股票评级 
买入 相对同期基准指数涨幅在 10%以上 
增持 相对同期基准指数涨幅在 5%~10%之间 
中性 相对同期基准指数涨幅在-5%~+5%之间 
减持 相对同期基准指数跌幅在 5%以上 
行业评级 
看好 相对表现优于市场 
中性 相对表现与市场持平 
看淡 相对表现弱于市场

### Page 1

Doanh thu 2Q2026 tăng trưởng chậm 
nhưng biên lợi nhuận gộp vẫn được 
duy trì do tăng giá bán

Sản lượng tiêu thụ 6M2026 chững lại 
do tâm lý nhập hàng thận trọng và 
hoạt động xây dựng phục hồi chậm

Biên lợi nhuận gộp được dự báo sẽ 
thu hẹp cuối năm, phản ánh chi phí 
nguyên liệu cao hơn trong 1Q2026

Khuyến nghị TRUNG LẬP, giá mục 
tiêu 166,100 VND/cổ phiếu 
 Doanh thu/LNST 2Q2026 đạt lần lượt 1,320/367 tỷ VND (+1%/+11% YoY). Lũy kế 
6M2026, doanh thu đạt 2,777 tỷ VND (+3% YoY) trong khi LNST ước đạt 671 tỷ VND 
(+9% YoY). Biên lãi gộp 6M2026 cải thiện đạt 47.0% (+240 bps YoY), chủ yếu do việc 
tăng giá bán giúp doanh nghiệp bù đắp được mức tăng của chi phí đầu vào.

Lũy kế 6M2026, sản lượng tiêu thụ của BMP ước đạt 43,920 tấn, giảm 5% svck. Việc 
BMP thu hẹp các chương trình chiết khấu, với mức chiết khấu bình quân khoảng 
5.2% trong 2Q2026, làm giảm động lực nhập hàng của các đại lý và khiến sản lượng 
tiêu thụ chậm lại. Dù vậy, thị phần toàn quốc của BMP ước đạt 28% (+1 đpt svck) 
nhờ các doanh nghiệp khác trong ngành gặp khó khăn khi giá hạt nhựa tăng mạnh.

Biên lợi nhuận gộp của BMP dự kiến thu hẹp trong 2H2026 trước áp lực chi phí 
nguyên liệu đầu vào tăng. Tuy nhiên, chúng tôi kỳ vọng mức độ ảnh hưởng sẽ thấp 
hơn so với các doanh nghiệp trong ngành do (1) các lô nguyên liệu giá cao từ 
1Q2026 đã được đưa vào sản xuất và tiêu thụ một phần trong 1H2026; (2) BMP chủ 
động nâng gấp đôi hàng tồn kho để ứng phó với biến động bất lợi của giá nguyên 
liệu; (3) hơn 90% nhu cầu nguyên liệu uPVC được cung ứng từ các đối tác trong 
nước như hệ sinh thái SCG tại Việt Nam, AGC Chemicals và LSP; và (4) công ty duy 
trì giá bán bình quân ở mức cao.

Dựa trên kết quả kinh doanh và định giá, chúng tôi khuyến nghị TRUNG LẬP với cổ 
phiếu BMP, giá mục tiêu là 166,100 VND/cổ phiếu (tương ứng với mức tăng 14.3% 
so với giá đóng cửa ngày 23/09/2026).

Tăng trưởng doanh thu và lợi nhuận thuần 5 năm gần nhất Hoạt động kinh doanh

Nhựa Bình Minh (BMP) là doanh nghiệp hàng đầu 
trong lĩnh vực sản xuất ống nhựa với thị phần 
dẫn đầu tại thị trường nội địa (27% vào cuối 
2023), thị trường chính tập trung tại khu vực 
miền Nam. BMP có 4 nhà máy với tổng công suất 
đạt 150,000 tấn/năm. 
Nguồn: BMP, KBSV

Điểm nhấn đầu tư  Chú thích 
Kỳ vọng đà phục hồi của xây dựng dân dụng và bất động sản miền Nam sẽ thúc đẩy sản lượng tiêu 
thụ tăng trưởng trong 2026–2027. 
 Chi tiết ở phần dưới của báo cáo 
Giá PVC được kỳ vọng sẽ hạ nhiệt và khả năng giữ giá bán đầu ra ở mức cao giúp BMP giữ biên lợi 
nhuận gộp ở nền cao. 
 Chi tiết ở phần dưới của báo cáo

### Page 4

4

Nhu cầu nhập hàng thận trọng và 
cắt giảm các đợt chiết khấu trong 
kỳ của BMP làm giảm sản lượng tiêu 
thụ trong 2Q2026

Sự hồi phục của nhu cầu xây dựng 
dân dụng và nguồn cung các dự án 
bất động sản tại miền Nam sẽ thúc 
đẩy tăng trưởng sản lượng 2H2026 
 Sản lượng tiêu thụ trong 2Q2026 ước đạt 20,100 tấn, giảm 11% svck, nâng tổng 
sản lượng tiêu thụ 6M2026 ước đạt 43,920 tấn, giảm 5% svck do các yếu tố bất 
lợi từ bên ngoài như (1) giá hạt nhựa tăng cao khiến thị trường dè chừng trong 
việc nhập hàng vì lo ngại nguồn cung bất ổn (2) BMP đã thu hẹp các chương 
trình chiết khấu so với 2025 ngay trước thời điểm xung đột bùng nổ, giúp tỷ lệ 
chi phí bán hàng/doanh thu về mức 11.3%, giảm 800 bps so với 1Q2026 ở mức 
19.3%, nhưng đồng thời cũng khiến sản lượng tiêu thụ của BMP suy giảm do 
chính sách bán hàng thắt chặt hơn với các đối thủ có thị phần thấp hơn.

Chúng tôi kỳ vọng sản lượng tiêu thụ của BMP dần hồi phục từ 3Q2026 khi nhu 
cầu nhập hàng của các đại lý cải thiện sau giai đoạn thận trọng trong nửa đầu 
năm. Về cuối năm, hoạt động xây dựng dự kiến tăng tốc khi điều kiện thi công 
thuận lợi hơn sau mùa mưa. Bên cạnh đó, việc triển khai các dự án nhà ở tại 
TP.HCM và các tỉnh lân cận được kỳ vọng hỗ trợ nhu cầu ống nhựa trong giai 
đoạn tới. Theo đó, chúng tôi dự phóng sản lượng tiêu thụ của BMP lần lượt đạt 
96,695/100,125/96,750 tấn trong 2026/2027/2028.

thu và lợi nhuận thuần/sản lượng tiêu thụ

Chí Minh

Nguồn: BMP, KBSV  Nguồn: CBRE, KBSV

Biểu đồ 5. Vòng quay hàng tồn kho của BMP so với các doanh nghiệp trong ngành 
   BMP duy trì vòng quay dẫn đầu trong 
ngành nhờ lợi thế nguồn nguyên liệu và thị 
phần đứng đầu tại miền Nam. Tuy nhiên, 
xu hướng suy giảm các quý gần đây về 
mức 5.3 lần, giảm 0.21 điểm QoQ do nhu 
cầu tiêu thụ toàn ngành suy giảm.

### Page 5

5

Lợi thế từ hệ sinh thái công ty mẹ 
SCG và đối tác Nhật Bản giúp BMP 
có nhiều dư địa để nâng cao hiệu 
quả hoạt động

Theo chia sẻ từ ban lãnh đạo, BMP có lợi thế vì nhập nguyên liệu từ công ty mẹ 
là hệ sinh thái SCG tại Việt Nam và đối tác Nhật Bản là AGC Chemicals Vietnam, 
giúp giảm được 5% thuế nhập khẩu và chi phí vận chuyển. Điều này giúp BMP 
hưởng lợi kép khi có thể tăng được giá bán theo thị trường nhưng nguyên vật 
liệu rẻ hơn so với các doanh nghiệp trong ngành. KBSV nhận thấy định hướng 
dài hạn của BMP là cân bằng lợi ích cổ đông và khách hàng, vì vậy việc giảm giá 
bán để giành thị phần không phải lựa chọn ưu tiên của doanh nghiệp mà là 
nâng cao tỷ suất lợi nhuận/sản phẩm thành phẩm.

KBSV dự báo biên lợi nhuận gộp thu hẹp trong 2H2026 khi chi phí nguyên liệu 
giá cao từ 1H2026 được phản ánh vào giá vốn, và hồi phục trong 2027–2028 
nhờ đặc tính giữ giá bán ít thay đổi của nhóm nhựa. Chúng tôi kỳ vọng giá PVC 
resin chịu áp lực giảm từ (1) nhu cầu xây dựng tại Trung Quốc còn yếu khi thị 
trường bất động sản chưa hồi phục rõ ràng; và (2) các nhà sản xuất giải phóng 
hàng tồn kho sau mùa mưa, làm tăng sản lượng hạt nhựa PVC resin. Theo đó, 
KBSV dự phóng biên lợi nhuận gộp của BMP lên 46.4%/46.7%/46.5% trong 
2026/2027/2028.

gộp của BMP

Document 301: Five ways the UK’s Autumn Budget could shock us

Source PDF: all report pdf/Five ways the UK’s Autumn Budget could shock us.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 10
Status: ok

### Page 1

From better ﬁscal headroom to surprise income tax hikes, this
is how UK Chancellor John Healey's ﬁrst budget could surprise
investors
James Smith
UK Chancellor John Healey
In a nutshell: What we expect
Higher debt interest costs look set to drive the level of 'ﬁscal
headroom' from £24bn in March to £10bn. There's a growing
sense that Chancellor John Healey would like to get this up
to £15bn or higher. The government is set to modestly
increase spending on the cost of living, though the total

intervention is unlikely to meaningfully top £5bn and it's
likely to be mostly billed as temporary to avoid counting
against the main ﬁscal rule. We expect an increase in capital
spending of roughly £10bn/year, bearing in mind this is
exempt from the ﬁscal rule dictating that the current budget
must be in surplus after ﬁve years. To rebuild headroom
Cookie settings

### Page 2

against that rule, we expect £5-6bn of tax hikes, likely via a
higher Bank Surcharge and possibly broadening the National
Insurance tax base.
In short, we don't expect the budget to deliver a material
stimulus (or add materially to the already-planned ﬁscal
consolidation). The impact on the Bank of England, which is
poised to hike rates in November, is likely to be fairly
minimal.
But all chancellors like to keep a rabbit in their hat – and what
follows are ﬁve ways it could surprise us.

1. Fiscal headroom goes up – not down
What if the UK’s ﬁscal hole isn’t nearly as bad as we think?
The widespread expectation going into the UK’s Autumn Budget on
28 October is that the Chancellor is going to have to face up to
higher debt interest costs with some modest tax hikes.
Bank of England rate expectations are almost a percentage point
higher than they were at the time of the Oﬃce for Budget
Responsibility’s March assessment of Britain’s public ﬁnances. 10-

year bond yields are some 70 basis points higher, which, taken
together, will push up debt interest spending by £11bn/year by
2029/30.
That points to a sizeable reduction in the amount of money the
government has left under its main ﬁscal rule, which requires the
current budget (day-to-day spending, including debt interest less
revenue) to be in surplus within three years. Back in March, that
surplus, or ﬁscal headroom, was projected at £24bn in 2029/30.

Debt interest costs are rising

### Page 3

*Based on the average rate in the 10 days up to 25 September, which
likely represents the OBR's observation window for this budget
Source: Macrobond, ING
We think that headroom will have dropped to around £10bn before
any corrective action. And the vibe from the Treasury suggests the
Chancellor would prefer it to be above £15bn, to give more
breathing space in case of future adverse forecast revisions. That
would point to some limited tax hikes.
But what if that’s all wrong? The truth is, nobody really knows how

the OBR will change its assessment of the UK economy. And as we
saw last year, the Treasury is often inclined to talk up the doom and
gloom to open up a positive surprise on the day. 12 months ago,
the hit to headroom was considerably smaller than had been
widely expected.
This time, any positive surprise is most likely to come from wage
growth. Will the rise in energy prices spill into higher pay, just as it
did in 2022? We think it won’t, not least because the jobs market is

much weaker, an assessment we suspect the OBR will share.
But even relatively small upgrades to wage growth can make a big
diﬀerence to the government’s fortunes. Since 2010, the OBR has
revised the level of earnings across its ﬁve-year forecast by an
average of 1.3 percentage points in absolute terms. If it did that
now, say by lifting wage growth over the next two years by 0.6ppt
apiece, it would add £10bn to headroom, almost perfectly
oﬀsetting the hit from higher interest costs.

### Page 4

There are also reasons to think the impact of lower migration
projections, which some reports suggest could wipe another £7bn
oﬀ headroom via lower tax revenue forecasts, could be much less
than feared. The starting point for employment looks a little higher
than it did back in March. The rise in global equities this year should
also be helpful for capital gains tax projections.
Why does this all matter? Because the higher the headroom, the
less ‘need’ there is to hike taxes, and if it is signiﬁcantly higher than

expected, the more room there is to increase spending under the
ﬁscal rules.
The simple message is this: don’t judge the Autumn Budget by the
level of headroom left over.
2. Spending splurge
The problem with ‘headroom’ is not only that it’s highly sensitive to
the OBR’s economic outlook, but that the forward-looking nature of
the ﬁscal rules opens the door to a lot of creative accounting.
The impact of higher inﬂation is a case in point. This helps the

Treasury through higher receipts – both VAT and income tax. Yes, it
also pushes up the cost of indexed beneﬁts. But importantly,
departmental budgets don’t automatically increase to oﬀset the
impact of higher prices.
That opens up a classic trick from the Treasury, which is to leave
spending plans virtually unchanged in nominal terms, setting up
substantially smaller real-terms budget increases across the
medium term.
Even before the Iran War, government plans pencilled in real-terms

cuts to per capita departmental spending in FY2029. Given some
functions, like health, are ‘protected’, that implies much deeper cuts
to smaller departments.
Suﬃce it to say, that is unrealistic. Higher inﬂation will push up the
wage bill across government. It would cost £12bn to simply
maintain those March departmental spending plans in real terms.
That’s before considering the cost of ending that austerity pencilled

Targeting Cookies

### Page 5

in for later this decade, or how the government will fund its defence
or social care ambitions.
A top-up to government departments is inevitable – the question is
when. It looks like the Treasury wants to kick the can on these tricky
budget decisions until next autumn, when it plans to release its
next Spending Review, a process of allocating money across
departments. No doubt the hope is that debt interest costs will
have fallen and the economic outlook brightened, such that there's

more space under the ﬁscal rules to make bigger spending
commitments.
That points to a very modest package of spending measures this
month, mainly targeting the cost of living. On top of the already-
announced cut to VAT on energy, we’d imagine the Treasury will
replicate last year’s budget and lower household bills by funding a
greater share of ‘policy costs’ from general taxation. This has the
added beneﬁt of lowering headline inﬂation at a particularly

sensitive moment for the Bank of England. Crucially though, the
government is likely to bill these moves as temporary, so they don’t
count under that main ﬁscal rule.
But if the economic picture really is better than feared, don’t rule
out the government going further. A decision to fully fund the UK’s
3% defence spending commitment would be a major wildcard,
though this would likely come at the expense of capital plans in
other areas of government.

Ultimately, the key thing to watch is the OBR’s assessment of how
all these policy changes aﬀect GDP growth and borrowing over the
next year. If there’s a material stimulus eﬀect, it could add to
market bets on UK rate hikes.
3. A manifesto break on income tax
Tax hikes are likely at the Autumn Budget, but should be very small.
We’re probably looking at a package of circa £5bn (0.2% of GDP).
Part of the problem is that the list of viable options looks perilously

small if Labour is to avoid breaking its manifesto pledge not to raise

the major taxes, which together account for around 70% of total
revenue.
Our best guess is that the budget will include a hike in the Bank
Surcharge (raising £2bn if increased from 3% to 8%), together with
a broadening of the tax base for National Insurance to landlords
and partners in LLPs (together yielding around £2bn/year). Other
options, like ending a loophole on stamp duty for commercial
property or lowering the VAT threshold for businesses, have also

been on the table.
The problem with many of these measures is that they require
massive changes to stand any chance of raising material sums,
risking big behavioural reactions while oﬀering uncertain revenue
potential. There’s a reason why former chancellor Rachel Reeves
shied away from many of the measures we outline in the chart
below.
How the Chancellor could raise taxes

Is there scope for a surprise? We think there are two main ways on
tax – one good, one bad.
The good? A surprise announcement of a so-called ‘tax switch’, a
novel proposal from Resolution Foundation which would see the
government simultaneously raise basic income tax and cut

employee National Insurance by 2ppt. The impact on workers
would be neutral, but because income tax has a broader base
(including pensions), this would raise £6bn/year.
Though not a substantial sum, it would signal to investors that
Prime Minister Andy Burnham isn’t afraid to go near the thorny
issue of raising taxes. But in light of recent pension changes,

### Page 8

including to the infamous ‘triple lock’, this feels like too much of a
political gamble at this stage.
The negative surprise would be if the Treasury decided to raise the
basic tax allowance. Burnham ﬂirted with this on his ﬁrst day in
oﬃce; it would not only be expensive (potentially £10bn), but it
would also signal that the UK’s commitment to the threshold freeze
– which is doing the lion's share of the planned ﬁscal consolidation
– is waning.
A big rise in the National Living Wage

The minimum wage increase in April 2027, which will be
announced alongside the budget, is perhaps the most important
variable for the Bank of England.
This is based on a recommendation from the Low Pay Commission,
which earlier this year provisionally estimated the increase at 3.7%.
That’s a smaller increase than over the past two years and,
crucially, is not a million miles above the level the Bank considers
consistent with a 2% medium-term inﬂation target (3.25%).

But the LPC has some ﬂexibility in its recommendation, and one
criterion is the cost of living. Higher inﬂation means we could see a
ﬁnal proposal between 4% and 4.5%. Anything above that would,
we suspect, be problematic for the Bank of England and would
bolster the case for higher rates.
The government is no doubt well aware of this. And there appears
to be growing recognition among Labour ﬁgures, including on the
left of the party, that the big minimum wage increases, coupled

with previous payroll tax increases, have had a big impact on
business – particularly in consumer services.
But if the LPC were to recommend minimum wage growth of close
to 5%, could the government resist it? In theory, yes. But politically,
we suspect not.
A surge in bond issuance
We already know that bond issuance is set to increase again in
2027, though for reasons unrelated to the UK’s ﬁscal trajectory.

The chart below shows that in April, the Debt Management Oﬃce
projected a gross ﬁnancing need of £308bn, similar to the big
number in FY2025. We suspect that when we get the updated DMO
remit following the budget, next year’s issuance could be closer to
£325bn – though a greater chunk of that will likely get funded using
bills, meaning the actual pick-up in gilt sales relative to those
previous plans is likely to be more modest.

How debt issuance plans broke down as of

Source: Oﬃce for Budget Responsibility, Debt Management Oﬃce, ING
Still, the point is that, beneath these numbers, public sector net
borrowing is still likely to be projected to keep falling through the
rest of the decade. The major risk – related to some of the things

discussed earlier, like larger near-term spending or surprise
increases in tax allowances – would jeopardise this fundamental
improvement in bond issuance in the medium term. That's
something investors wouldn’t take kindly to.

增113%，營收占比達21.6%。隨高附加價值產品比重增加，1H26毛利率及營業利益率分別提升至
22.4%及7.0%，顯示公司成長動能正由營收規模擴張，進一步轉化為獲利提升。

### Page 22

全球生產基地加速擴產，效益將於2026年起逐步顯現
◼ 泰國一廠：
◼ 主要生產高階iHDI、HLC及光模塊產品，於3Q25進入量產。
◼ 量產爬坡順利，已通過多家客戶認證及量產，尚有多家伺服器與光模塊全球領先客戶正積極進行認證中。
◼ 7月已達損益兩平。
◼ 目前另有三棟新廠房及一棟機械鑽孔中心同步建置中，規劃將於4Q26起逐步進入試產階段。
◼ 淮安園區：
◼ 規劃至2026年底，淮安HA、HB、HC園區將有6棟高階PCB新廠完成建設。
◼ 另HD園區已於今年四月動土，規劃新建HDI/MSAP與HLC廠，8月10日封頂，預計明年三月加入生產。
◼ 深圳園區：
◼ 第三園區智慧製造基地於今年七月動土，預計興建三棟智慧製造廠房，重點布局AI伺服器用及光模塊用的
高階類載板，及AI終端用高階軟板等高端產品。
◼ 禮鼎深圳第二園區於今年九月正式動土，總投資金額不低於人民幣120億元，規劃建設數座工廠，聚焦
FCBGA等高階IC載板。新產能主要面向AI、高效能運算及先進封裝需求，進一步強化公司高階FCBGA產品的

研發、製造與規模化交付能力。

致力推動EPS＋ESG，國際ESG評比持續提升
► 標普全球企業永續評鑑 S&P Global ESG
2025年S&P Global ESG評級進步至88分，並入選道瓊領先世界
指數 (DJBIC World) 及道瓊領先新興市場指數 (DJBIC Emerging
Markets)，亦於2022年至2025年連續4年入選標普全球永續年
鑑 (Sustainability Yearbook)。
► 臺灣公司治理評鑑
2025年公司治理評鑑排名上市公司6%-20%，再度入選臺灣
證券交易所公司治理100指數。
► Sustainalytics ESG 風險評級
最新Sustainalytics ESG風險評級為16.1，為低等風險等級。
► ISS ESG 評級
最新年度的ISS ESG評級為「C+」，獲得「優秀 (Prime)」
等級。
► CDP 碳揭露專案
2025年在氣候變遷與水安全管理皆獲得「A」最高等級殊
榮，其中氣候變遷為首度獲得A級，水安全則已連續兩年獲

得A級。
► 富時羅素 FTSE Russell ESG
最新年度的FTSE Russell ESG評級獲得4.5分（滿分為5分），
成為台灣PCB企業第一名。

Document 401: Webinar_ Europe’s investment landscape - what’s changing and why it matters

Source PDF: all report pdf/Webinar_ Europe’s investment landscape - what’s changing and why it matters.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 2
Status: ok

### Page 1

Join ING economists to explore new research that challenges
perceptions of Europe's investment culture. We'll debunk some
common misconceptions, examine how attitudes and
behaviours are changing, and discuss what it all means for
Europe's future growth. Sign up here
Marieke Blom, Carsten Brzeski, Charlotte de Montpellier, Amrita
Naik Nimbalkar, Sebastian Franke, Paolo Pizzoli, Valentin Tataru
and Rebecca Byrne
Europe is often seen as a continent of cautious savers. But the story

is more nuanced than commonly believed. New ING research
suggests the gap between European and US savings is smaller than
often assumed, while attitudes towards investing may be shifting.
Half of Europeans with savings already invest, and another 30%
say they would consider doing so.
In this webinar, we'll discuss why this matters for household wealth
and economic growth, what is preventing more Europeans from
investing, and how attitudes diﬀer across countries. We'll also

explore what could help to unlock Europe's investment potential.

WESTPAC MARKET OUTLOOK
October 2026
CONTENTS

Westpac Market Outlook is a monthly publication
produced by Westpac Economics.
Internet: www.westpac.com.au
Email: economics@westpac.com.au
This issue was finalised on 15 October 2026.

### Page 3

Past performance is not a reliable indicator of future performance. The forecasts given above are predictive in character. Whilst every effort has been taken to ensure that the assumptions on which the forecasts are based are reasonable, 
the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
Markets have had little respite over the past month. Hopes 
of a lasting US–Iran agreement have faded and intermittent 
attacks on shipping have kept Brent oil prices in triple digits. 
Workarounds have allowed a meaningful share of crude to 
continue flowing but these arrangements are slower and 
costlier, while depleted inventories leave little room to 
absorb another shock and a range of disruptions to refining 
and shipping are also impacting downstream energy costs. 
Bond markets have been just as restless, with continued lifts 
in government bond yields across much of the globe and 
more sharply in the US. 
Notably, oil is no longer doing the heavy lifting for yields. 
The rise has been concentrated in real yields, with the Fed’s 
September hike – and its signal of more to come – acting as 
something of a trigger. In the US, fiscal concerns have been 
more of a sub-plot, though the Treasury’s efforts to hold 
down long yields have arguably only drawn more attention 
to them. 
In our view, markets are getting ahead of themselves on 
monetary policy offshore. We expect just one more hike 
from the Fed rather than the three the market currently has 
priced, with stretched housing affordability posing risks to 
both consumption and inflation. Central banks in Europe, the 
UK and Canada are also likely to bide their time after a final 
move. Europe is weathering the energy shock better than 
feared and has far more fiscal room to respond than the US – 
one reason we still see the US dollar trending lower over the 
next two years.
The RBA hiked again in September and looks set to follow 
up in November, taking the cash rate to a new cycle high. 
Some of the risks the Board flagged have crystallised, 
but the minutes also reveal a tendency to reach for the 
more hawkish read of the data, in addition to the familiar 
downbeat take on supply capacity. Our base case assumes 
no return to full-scale conflict, but with energy buffers 
depleted, bond markets less forgiving and the RBA already 
leaning hawkish, the margin for error – for markets and 
policymakers alike – is starting to ‘wear thin’.
Australia: After a soft but better than expected first half, the 
Australian economy is facing into more challenges as another 
round of disruptions from global energy markets combine with 
further interest rate rises and a deepening housing downturn. 
The demand mix should see capacity pressures continue to 
ease but with significant risks on either side.
Middle East: Low inventories and higher shipping costs will 
keep oil and fuel prices elevated, with Brent futures forecast 
to average US$101/bbl in Q4 before easing to US$77/bbl by 
Q4 2027 as supply recovers. Risks are two-sided: escalation 
could push Brent towards US$150/bbl, while an early US-Iran 
agreement could see a faster normalisation in supply.
Commodities: Most commodities rose in September, with 
many of the themes that drove moves across August remaining 
in place. Japanese LNG and Brent oil recorded strong gains 
amid Middle East tensions. Copper and aluminium extended 
recent gains while iron ore was supported by higher freight 
and production costs. Gold fell after the Fed’s September rate 
hike. 
Global FX Markets: In late September and early-October 
the DXY index popped higher to near 103, a high back to 
early-2025. Underlying the move are expectations that 
inflation will prove persistent globally as well as a belief that 
the US’ tech sector leaves it best positioned to weather the 
storm.
New Zealand: Higher fuel costs and a weaker exchange rate 
have lifted the short-term inflation outlook while the economy 
has continued to grow through the Iran war. Growth looks set 
to continue to be buoyed by strong commodity prices and a 
supportive exchange rate. However, downside risks are growing 
from higher energy costs and long-term interest rates. 
United States: The US housing market remains under 
considerable pressure. Prices continue to edge up, supporting 
wealth. But up-and-coming main street consumers are 
struggling with affordability and availability, posing enduring 
risks for both consumption growth and inflation.   
China: With each month that passes, the risk of disappointing 
authorities’ 4.5-5.0% ambition grows. While the Government 
is right to take a targeted approach to support, it must quickly 
multiply its scale if downside risks are to be kept at bay. 
Asia: For much of the past twenty years, Asia has been a 
source of disinflation for the world. But a combination of ageing 
populations, rising labour costs, increasing electricity demand 
and technology sector developments imply Asia may provide 
less support to global disinflation over the decade ahead.
Europe & UK: The euro has come under considerable pressure 
during the past month. That said, the European economy 
continues to tick along nicely while inflation risks remain 
manageable, allowing the ECB to be measured in their response.

Wearing thin

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FINANCIAL MARKETS
The main development affecting global markets this month
has been the sharp lift in selected bond yields, especially in
the US and France. Since the previous Market Outlook was
published, 10-year benchmarks have risen 27bps for US

Treasuries, to be well above the 5% threshold, and 40bps for
French bonds. The latter move contrasts with German bunds,
which have seen little change in yields at that tenor over the
month, though they are up more than 60bps year to date.
Several factors are behind the latest rise, with no single
cause dominating. For some of these factors, the underlying
conditions had been in evidence for some time, but recent
events focused attention on them. The rise has been primarily

a story of real yields: longer-run inflation expectations implied
by both breakevens and swaps have not increased that much.
For much of the year, fluctuations in oil prices – and thus the
near-term inflation outlook – fed into market views of central
bank policy, and longer yields as well. More recently, though,
this correlation has broken down. Shifts in expectations about
central bank policy have remained important for bond pricing,
as evident in the concentration of the increase in real yields.

The FOMC’s decision to increase the Fed funds rate, and flag in
the ‘dot plots’ that at least one more hike was likely, seems to
have been something of a trigger event for repricing, not only
for US yields. Also salient were the various actions taken by
the US Treasury to suppress long yields and improve the US
government’s debt-servicing position. These efforts had little
lasting effect and yields are now higher, and higher relative to
peers, than before. But they may have served to draw attention

to the growing issues around US fiscal sustainability. High
supply of debt from hyperscaler firms to fund the AI / data
centre boom has also likely pressured yields at the margin.
That said, fiscal concerns are unlikely to have been the main
issue driving the lift in US yields. The slope of the US yield
curve is noticeably flatter than at the beginning of the year;
in other words, short yields have risen even more than the
10-year yield, signifying tighter expected monetary policy

lifting yields across the curve. Another lens on this shift is that
2-year and other short-dated yields have increased more than
estimates of risk-neutral rate, such as that from the NY Fed.
Fiscal concerns have been more salient in the case of France,
where spreads to bunds have widened from around 70bps at
the beginning of the year to nearly 140bps currently, wider than
Greece or Italy, with about 40bps of the widening occurring in
the past month. Contagion to other euro area sovereigns has

been limited, with spreads widening around 22bps over the
month for Greece, Italy and Belgium, and 13bps for Spain.
Many factors lifting global bond yields ...
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group

### Page 5

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
5
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FINANCIAL MARKETS
... and RBA policy rates outlook
In Australia, the RBA hiked the cash rate again at its late
September meeting, and a further hike at the November
meeting looks likely. This would take the cash rate to 4.85%, a
new cycle high.
While the forecasts the RBA published in August did not

support further rate hikes, in September these forecasts were
characterised as resting on several things going right and
certain risks not emerging. Some of these risks, including a re-
escalation of the war in the Middle East and thus energy prices,
have since crystallised. The post-meeting communication
and minutes also pointed to the July CPI result being above
expectations, as was June quarter GDP growth “at the margin”.
The RBA is also wary about pressure on both construction

costs and retail prices of IT goods arising from the AI/data
centre boom. No weight is put on the possibility that this new
investment and technology might add to productivity or the
capital stock, and so supply capacity, even with a lag. Indeed,
the post-meeting minutes highlighted the financial stability
risks if valuations in this area should disappoint “even if the
technology were ultimately successful”, as if the latter was not
yet clear. This language points to a generally downbeat view of

Australian supply capacity that also emerges in its discussion
of productivity trends. Although its position piece in the August
2025 Statement on Monetary Policy  indicated that lower trend
growth in productivity had no implications for inflation or
monetary policy, more recent statements imply that it does.
More broadly, the minutes for the September meeting revealed
a tendency to contemplate more hawkish interpretations
of data and outcomes. For example, the minutes dismissed

evidence of the labour market easing a bit more than
was forecast by pointing to the changes to the survey
arrangements. At the same time, the minutes contemplated
that the rate of unemployment consistent with inflation
being at target might have risen, even though wages growth
has marginally undershot the RBA’s forecasts and the cost
pressures cited by its business liaison contacts are mostly
non-labour costs. The minutes also included an extended

discussion of the possibility that the global neutral interest
rate might have increased recently. If true, this would imply
that financial conditions were less restrictive. This conclusion
would be hard to square with yield curves that have flattened
since the beginning of the year and other central bank
estimates of the term premium having increased, both of which
suggest tighter financial conditions.
That said, the RBA will find it hard to look past inflation

outcomes in the second half of year that is likely to remain
sticky. We anticipate trimmed mean inflation to record
quarterly outcomes of around 0.9%qtr in Q3 and Q4, remaining
around the current 3½%yr pulse. The Monetary Policy Board
will want to see clear evidence of inflation coming down before
withdrawing policy restrictiveness, so we continue to pencil in
August as the likely date for start of the rate-cutting phase.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUSTRALIAN ECONOMY
After posting a soft but better than expected first half, the
Australian economy is facing into more challenges near term
as another round of disruptions from global energy markets
combine with a further rise in interest rates and a deepening

housing downturn. Against this, business investment is in
the midst of a sustained ramp-up led by a large and growing
pipeline of data centre and energy investment projects.
Elsewhere, public demand is growing at a slower pace and
new dwelling investment is likely to moderate but both are
expected to still see positive growth. Overall, the demand
mix should see capacity pressures continue to ease but with
significant risks on either side.
Shifting political winds

Some of these risks are now in the wider policy space. The
political landscape has changed materially since the 2025
Federal Election with opinion polls showing a sustained swing
against the Labor government and a marked rise in support
for One Nation that raises the prospect of a three-horse race
in the next election, the outcome of which could be very hard
to predict (we may get a preview of this with next month’s
Victorian state election). While the next Federal election is

still a long way off – not due until well into 2028 – the shift
does appear to be having some bearing on policy. For example,
the government’s choice not to reinstate the temporary
halving in fuel excise tax that was deployed when fuel prices
first spiked back in March suggests it is less inclined towards
providing broad based cost-of-living support that might make
it harder to bring inflation back down.
Migration policy is shaping more clearly as a battleground.

A suite of changes announced by the government in mid-
September and which came into effect in October, are already
expected to slow working holidaymaker and foreign student
arrivals, moderating total net overseas migration from
around 300k current to 225k by 2028, trimming population
growth from around 1.4%yr to 1.2%yr. For its part, the main
Liberal-National opposition party has proposed to reduce net
migration to 100k in the first two years of government, before

increasing to 130k and 160k in the two years following –
which would likely see total population growth slow to around
0.7-0.8%yr initially. One Nation has proposed a period of ‘net
negative’ migration, lowering net migration by 250k a year
over the first three years of government before considering a
‘ceiling’ of 130k per year thereafter. This would see population
growth slow to around 0.2-0.3%yr initially.
While none of these policies are on the table for this year

or next, the direction of travel is clear with a risk that the
current government moves further towards curbing migrant
More challenges near term ...
Matthew Hassan, Head of Australian Macro-Forecasting
inflows. Slower population growth basically maps one-to-
one to GDP growth although the specific flow-on impacts to
areas like housing and labour markets are more complex and
will depend on the population mix and how the demand and
supply effects balance out. For the record, we have trimmed

our own population growth forecasts in the 2028 out-year,
from 1.4%yr to 1.2%yr with a related tweak to our GDP
growth forecast for that year.
Consumer sentiment-spending gap
Near term challenges continue to centre on the consumer.
Here, the latest updates show sentiment again plumbing
historical lows but more mixed messages around spending
which is still holding up better than might be expected based
on a simple read on the consumer mood.
Certainly that mood remains deeply negative. The Westpac–

Melbourne Institute Consumer Sentiment Index dropped
4.7% to 80.4 in October, putting the month amongst the forty
worst reads since the began in the early 1970s, following
two others reads in this group back in April and June and
another eight recorded in 2022–23. Altogether this marks the
worst period of recurring extremely weak sentiment since the
disastrous recession in the early 1990s.
This has been difficult to square with the soft rather than

outright weak picture on consumer spending through most
of this year. Even when real spending was recording more
material per capita declines back in 2023-24, these were
somewhat milder than what might have been expected given
the weakness in sentiment at the time.
We explored this more fully in our August Red Book . Some
of the disconnect may be a by-product of high inflation which
‘hits different’ sentiment-wise. Cost of living pressures,

### Page 7

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUSTRALIAN ECONOMY
... centred on uneven consumer, housing downturn ...
which are the central issue for consumers at the moment,
represent a broad-based but shallower squeeze rather than
the deeper shocks that typically come when big economic
downturns hit. Labour markets appear to be an important

point of distinction – despite the weakness in sentiment,
consumers have been relatively unperturbed about the jobs
outlook in recent years. Adding consumers’ unemployment
expectations to sentiment measures significantly improves its
‘fit’ as a predictor of per capita spending.
There other aspect that helps to reconcile the sentiment-
spending gap is the detailed picture across segments. There
may be one economy but there is not a single consumer.

Instead both sentiment and spending outcomes reflect the
average across a range of often quite varied conditions in
different consumer segments.
This shows through in the dispersion of sentiment across
sub-groups. The chart right shows the current distribution of
index reads across the 100-odd sub-groups we regularly track
with some selected groups called out. If the range seems wide
its because it is – measures of spread over the last two years
have been 50-60% wider than the average over the previous

three decades. Exactly why is not completely clear but the
observations point to a more fractured consumer with multiple
fault lines – including across states, metro/regional areas, voter
groups, labour force status, income levels and housing tenures.
Spending-wise, one of the clearest emerging fault lines is across
age groups. Here, there look to be three main ‘blocs’ – 18-34 year
olds (mainly millennials’); 35-54 year olds (mainly ‘gen X’); and

those aged over 55 (dominated by the ‘baby boomers’).
Demographically, the 55+ age group is both the largest and
the fastest-growing – our estimates suggest it now makes up
about 45% of all households, up 8.5pts compared to 2020. It
is also seeing significant financial shifts with the cohort coming
through carrying materially larger superannuation holdings
(reflecting a variety of factors including a longer period of
compulsory contributions that have earned compounding

returns) and more housing wealth. Again, our rough estimates
suggest average household wealth in this group is around
$2.5m up over $800k since 2020 (reflecting both the
aforementioned demographic shifts and valuation changes).
At the same time, the group has low debt levels – it is largely
untouched by interest rate increases and probably benefits
slightly insofar as this lifts interest income both directly and
indirectly via super holdings.
For 18-34 year olds, incomes are more important than balance

sheets, meaning inflation poses more of a constraint. For 35-
54 year olds, income is less of a constraint but high debt levels
poses a big challenge as rate rises increase servicing costs.
All of which is to say that it should come as little surprise that
spending by the 55+ age group has been running stronger
than the other age groups. Breakdowns from our Westpac
Consumer Panel show average non fuel spend has been rising
at a 5% annual pace over the last six months amongst those

Past performance is not a reliable indicator of future performance. The forecasts given above are predictive in character. Whilst every effort has been taken to ensure that the assumptions on which the forecasts are based are reasonable, 
the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUSTRALIAN ECONOMY
aged 55+. That compares to a more sedate 2.7-2.9% pace 
across the other age groups, which given the current pace of 
inflation, implies small declines in real, inflation-adjusted terms.
These demographic contrasts also account for the observed 
strength in key bellwether spending categories like new 
vehicle purchases. The total number of new car purchases 
by consumers is up close to 4%yr, led by strong growth in 
EVs. Notably, industry figures suggest the purchase of used 
cars is down sharply, by close to 9%yr (used car sales are 
not well covered in the statistics and largely bypass official 
ABS measures because the are mainly transfers between 
households rather than a new addition). 
Pulling these clues together, at least some of the resilience in 
spending looks to be a form of ‘wealth effect’ where balance 
sheet considerations are more important than the real purchasing 
power of incomes. While the impact of employment, wages, 
inflation and interest rate changes will still likely be the dominant 
driver of shifts in spending growth, the nature of this wealth 
effect and how it evolves from here could be a key swing factor. 
Housing downturn deepens
And where that swing factor may start to turn is in response 
to a deepening downturn in Australia’s housing market. The 
combined impact of interest rate rises and major changes to 
housing-related tax policy announced in the Federal budget is 
continuing to weigh heavily. We have been flagging for some 
time that uncertainty could see a more significant ‘air pocket’ 
emerge with very thin trading conditions and a sharper move 
lower in prices. That risk is crystallising with turnover already 
down 20% and prices off 8%. 
With the September interest rate rise and an expected hike 
in November still to come, more weakness is likely near term. 
We revised our house price forecasts lower with a decline of 
around 8% now expected for calendar 2026, with a peak-to-
trough decline of just over 10%. 
... wealth effects an uncertain swing factor 
This constitutes a more material housing downturn. The 
peak to trough fall is set to be biggest since the beginning of 
the national price measures in 1980, although prices in real, 
inflation-adjusted terms recorded larger falls during the early-
1990s and early-1980s recessions with capital city measures 
also suggesting there were larger real price falls during the 
recessions in the mid-1970s and early 1960s. 
It also points to a bigger housing-related drag on growth. 
As discussed previously, the main channels are through 
new dwelling investment, turnover and wealth effects. New 
dwelling investment is still carrying solid momentum (the 
number of dwelling under construction up 10% over the year 
to June) but is expected to see a slightly more pronounced 
slowing. Turnover is also expected to be slightly weaker. 
However, it is the wealth effects from price declines that is the 
area to watch. Our rough estimates suggest this will account 
for most of the –0.8ppts on annual GDP growth over the 
year to June 2027. The emphasis here though is on ‘rough’. 
Wealth effects have seeming disappeared for long periods 
and their precise timing is very unclear, e.g. how should the 
drag from the current downturn be viewed against what 
were presumably strong positives coming off the much larger 
preceding gains? Some of this could be due to ‘assymmetric’ 
behaviour, with effects playing out very differently when there 
wealth is rising or falling.
On balance, we expect the impact to see consumer spending 
‘muddle through’ rather than fall back to the weaker growth 
seen during the more intense cost of living and interest 
rate shocks in 2023-24. This partly reflects the indirect 
support coming from growth more generally – the AI-related 
investment boom, which we have covered at length in previous 
reports, a key factor helping to hold GDP growth up around 
1.5%yr and prevent a more material weakening in labour 
market conditions. For the consumer though this will continue 
to feel like a struggle with growth flat in per capita terms 
and a shakier picture around housing that will play out very 
differently for some segments.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
MIDDLE EAST
Low inventories and higher shipping costs will keep oil and
fuel prices elevated, with Brent futures forecast to average
US$101/bbl in Q4 before easing to US$77/bbl by Q4 2027
as supply recovers. Refined fuels and LNG prices should
ease more slowly. Risks are two-sided: escalation could

push Brent towards US$150/bbl, while an early US-Iran
agreement could see a faster normalisation in supply with
prices returning close to pre-conflict levels by end-2027.
Brent crude is back above US$100/bbl on fading confidence
in a lasting US-Iran agreement and intermittent attacks on
shipping. Buffers remain depleted, with the IEA estimating
global observed inventories were 507mb lower at the end of
August than at the start of the conflict.
This comes despite crude exports through the Strait of Hormuz

recovering to around 70% to 90% of pre-conflict levels in late
September. Saudi and UAE exports through the Yanbu and
Fujairah pipelines continue, with the East-West pipeline back
to normal after the early September attacks. Tanker crossings
have risen, although transponders remain switched off, leaving
visible transit at 1% to 4% of pre-conflict levels, while cargo
transfers in the Gulf of Oman have increased.
However, these workarounds are slower and more costly.

Ship-to-ship transfers can take 5 to 10 days, US escorts cause
delays and some tankers are taking longer routes. Fewer ships
are also available, with many redeployed to other markets.
Higher insurance premiums are adding to costs, with Gulf-to-
China tanker rates above US$0.9mn a day, around 15x higher
than a year ago. With prompt barrels scarce, the gap between
physical and futures prices widened again, with Dated Brent
around US$136/bbl in early October against US$101/bbl for

front-month futures.
Oil and fuel prices push higher ...
Sian Fenner, Head of Business & Industry Economics
Justin Smirk, Senior Economist
Luka Belobajdic, Economist
Refined prices have risen more than crude
Refined product markets were already tight going into the
conflict. Reduced Gulf supply, exacerbated by Ukrainian strikes
on Russian refineries, has added to that tightness. Together
with higher shipping costs and rising Dated Brent prices,
Singapore Gasoil 10ppm (diesel) has roughly doubled since the

Strait closed, compared with around 40% for crude futures.
Consequently, Singapore gasoil crack spread widened to
an average of around US$36.1/bbl in early October, from
US$17.5/bbl across 2025. Alongside the government’s excise
tax cut returning to normal, this has lifted national diesel and
petrol prices to around A$2.83/L and A$2.41/L.
Outlook
We do not see a lasting US-Iran agreement until at least
mid-2027. In the meantime, we assume there will not be a

return to full-scale military intervention. However, periodic
Iranian attacks on ships and facilities are expected to continue,
as will the US blockade of Iranian exports, keeping a supply
risk premium in place. We look for Brent futures to average
around US$101/bbl in Q4, then ease gradually as demand
from refiners and governments to rebuild stocks partly offsets
recovering supply. As the premium fades and the market
moves into surplus in late 2027, we forecast Brent at around

US$77/bbl in Q4 2027, easing towards US$70/bbl in 2028 as
Middle East production fully recovers.
Oil balance to gradually improve over 2027
Global oil supply is expected to rise by 4.0mb/d (4.0%) to
103.7mb/d in 2027, following a 6.1% fall in 2026. Most of this
reflects a gradual recovery in Gulf output, with Houthi attacks
on Red Sea shipping keeping Saudi production below its
